Deligne pairing for equidimensional morphisms
Shiquan Li

Abstract
. Let S S be a noetherian normal scheme, and let X → S X\to S be a surjective projective morphism of pure relative dimension d d . We construct a symmetric multi-additive functor 𝒫 ​ ic ⁡ ( X ) d + 1 → 𝒫 ​ ic ⁡ ( S ) \cPic(X)^{d+1}\to\cPic(S) , and prove its functorial properties. Our construction uses Elkik’s and García’s ideas, as well as algebraic Hartogs’ theorem. Moreover, our results can be used to define arithmetic intersection theory of hermitian line bundles for equidimensional morphisms.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
14C17, 14F06, 14G40

1.
Introduction

Intersection theory is a fundamental tool in algebraic geometry. For a
d
-dimensional scheme
X
proper over a field
k
, we can define the intersection number
(\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Pic^{d}(X)\to\mathbb{Z}
, which is a symmetric multi-additive group homomorphism. Then we want to generalize intersection number and construct an intersection theory for a family of schemes
X\to S
. Let
\cPic(X)
denote the category of invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules with morphisms being isomorphisms (we call it a
Picard category
), and similarly for
\cPic(S)
. For a smooth projective morphism
X\to S
of pure relative dimension 1, i.e. a smooth projective family of curves, Deligne
[
Del73
]
(Formulaire 1.3.16) defined a functorial pairing
\cPic(X)\times\cPic(X)\to\cPic(S)
. Then Deligne
[
Del87
]
conjectured that we could construct a functorial map (which we now call
Deligne pairing
)
\cPic(X)^{d+1}\to\cPic(S)
for a flat proper morphism
X\to S
of pure relative dimension
d
. Since then, Deligne pairing has been constructed for various families
X\to S
by a series of work such as
[
Elk89
]
,
[
Gar00
]
,
[
Duc05
]
,
[
Nak10
]
,
[
Dol22
]
, and
[
EF24
]
. These results impose different restrictions on the family
X\to S
. For example,
[
Gar00
]
requires
X\to S
to be a surjective equidimensional projective morphism of noetherian schemes of finite Tor-dimension, and
[
EF24
]
drops the noetherian assumption and requires
X\to S
to be a faithfully flat locally projective morphism of finite presentation.

Deligne pairing can also be viewed as the generalization of norm functor. For algebraic integer ring extensions, we have the norm map. In EGAII
[
GD61
]
§6.5, Grothendieck generalized the norm map to a norm functor
\cPic(X)\to\cPic(S)
for a flat finite morphism
X\to S
, which is exactly the Deligne pairing for a flat proper morphism
X\to S
of pure relative dimension
0
.

In EGAII
[
GD61
]
§6.5, Grothendieck defined another norm functor
\cPic(X)\to\cPic(S)
for a finite morphism
X\to S
to a normal scheme
S
, while the morphism is not necessarily flat. Inspired by Deligne pairing for flat morphisms, we generalize this norm functor to a symmetric multi-additive functor for equidimensional morphisms over normal schemes. We still call our construction
Deligne pairing
since for flat morphisms, our construction is identical to the original Deligne pairing.

Convention

All rings in this paper are commutative with identity
1
. We sometimes use “
=
” to denote a canonical isomorphism. For a scheme
X
, we use
X^{(1)}
to denote the set of points of codimension 1 in
X
, i.e.
X^{(1)}=\{x\in X\mid\dim\mathcal{O}_{X,x}=1\}
. We always use
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
to denote the relative dimension of a morphism. We follow the convention that
\dim(\emptyset)=-1
. In particular, a set of non-negative dimension is tacitly nonempty.

1.1.
Deligne pairing for equidimensional morphisms

Definition 1.1
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a morphism of schemes. We say that
f
is
of pure relative dimension

d
if for each
x\in X
, every irreducible component of
f^{-1}(f(x))
has dimension
d
. Such morphism is also called an
equidimensional morphism
.

For example, a flat morphism between varieties (integral schemes of finite type over a field) is equidimensional.

We state our main result as follows.

Theorem 1.2

(Theorem
7.4
,
5.3
,
5.5
,
5.6
,
5.7
,
5.8
,
4.27
)
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a surjective projective morphism of noetherian schemes of pure relative dimension
d
. Suppose that
S
is normal. Then we can define a symmetric multi-additive functor,

\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}\colon\cPic(X)^{d+1}\to\cPic(S)

which we call Deligne pairing, and satisfies the following functorial properties:

(1) Let
g\colon S^{\prime}\to S
be a good base change (defined in Definition
5.2
),
X^{\prime}=X\times_{S}S^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\to X
. Then we have a natural isomorphism

\langle g^{\prime*}\mathcal{L}_{1},g^{\prime*}\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,g^{\prime*}\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}}\to g^{*}\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}.

(2) Suppose that
S
is integral. Let
\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d}
be invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules, and let
\delta
be their intersection number on the generic fiber of
X\to S
. Let
\mathcal{M}
be an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module. Then we have a natural isomorphism

\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,f^{*}\mathcal{M},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d}\rangle_{X/S}\to\mathcal{M}^{\otimes\delta},

and the isomorphism is compatible with any good base change
S^{\prime}\to S
, where
S^{\prime}
is another noetherian integral normal scheme.

(3) Let
g\colon Y\to X
be a surjective projective morphism of noetherian schemes of pure relative dimension
d^{\prime}
. Suppose that
X
and
S
are normal. Then we have a natural isomorphism

\langle g^{*}\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,g^{*}\mathcal{L}_{d},\mathcal{M}_{d+1},\ldots,\mathcal{M}_{d+d^{\prime}+1}\rangle_{Y/S}\\
\to\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d},\langle\mathcal{M}_{d+1},\ldots,\mathcal{M}_{d+d^{\prime}+1}\rangle_{Y/X}\rangle_{X/S},

and the isomorphism is compatible with any good base change
S^{\prime}\to S
.

(4) Suppose that
d\geqslant 1
, and let
D
be an effective divisor on
X
such that
D\to S
is of pure relative dimension
d-1
. Then we have a natural isomorphism

\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{O}(D),\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d}\rangle_{X/S}\to\langle\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{D},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d}|_{D}\rangle_{D/S},

where we just delete
\mathcal{O}(D)
in the right expression. Moreover, this isomorphism is compatible with any good base change
S^{\prime}\to S
.

(5) Let
g\colon Y\to X
be a projective morphism. Suppose that
Y\to S
is a surjective projective morphism of pure relative dimension
d
, and that there exists an open subset
U\subseteq X
which is dense in each fiber of
f
and satisfies that
g\colon g^{-1}(U)\to U
is an isomorphism. Then we have a natural isomorphism

\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}\to\langle g^{*}\mathcal{L}_{1},g^{*}\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,g^{*}\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{Y/S}.

(6) If
d=0
, then
\langle\mathcal{L}\rangle_{X/S}=\textnormal{Nm}_{X/S}(\mathcal{L})
, where
\textnormal{Nm}_{X/S}
denotes the norm functor for a finite morphism over a normal scheme defined in
[
GD61
]
§6.5 (which we will review in the next section).

Moreover, if
f
is flat, then our Deligne pairing is identical to the original Deligne pairing defined by Deligne
[
Del73
]
, Elkik
[
Elk89
]
, García
[
Gar00
]
, Ducrot
[
Duc05
]
, Eriksson and Freixas
[
EF24
]
, etc.

Now we briefly review the history of Deligne pairing
1
1

1

For clarity, we may omit some minor conditions of the theorems in the review.
. In
[
GD61
]
§6.5, Grothendieck defined two kinds of norm functors
\textnormal{Nm}_{X/S}\colon\cPic(X)\to\cPic(S)
for a finite morphism
f\colon X\to S
: one is for the case that
f
is flat, and the other is for the case that
S
is normal.

For the flat case, there is an extensive literature on the norm functor and its generalization, Deligne pairing for flat morphisms. Deligne
[
Del73
]
constructed this Deligne pairing for
d=1
, and speculated a similar functor for general
d
. We list some important results for general
d
below. Elkik
[
Elk89
]
developed a method and constructed Deligne pairing for Cohen–Macaulay flat projective morphisms. Her student, García
[
Gar00
]
, modified her method and constructed Deligne pairing for equidimensional projective morphisms of finite Tor-dimension (which implies Deligne pairing for flat projective morphisms). Moreover, Ducrot
[
Duc05
]
used a different method and constructed Deligne pairing for flat projective morphisms. The above constructions are all over noetherian or locally noetherian schemes. Recently, Eriksson and Freixas
[
EF24
]
modified Ducrot’s method and constructed Deligne pairing for flat projective morphisms without assuming the schemes to be noetherian or locally noetherian.

For the second norm functor, on the contrary, there is few research on the topic before. Besides Grothendieck’s norm functor, we only knew that N. Nakayama constructed an intersection sheaf in
[
Nak10
]
. However, he needed stronger assumptions to prove that his intersection sheaf was invertible, and he did not consider functorial properties except a base change property. In addition, his construction is only a map between Picard groups and not a functor between Picard categories. And his proof used very involved algebraic geometry and commutative algebra, while our proof is more comprehensible with explicit geometric pictures. Nakayama’s results have many applications in algebraic geometry concerning Chow reductions and maximal rationally connected fibrations, which shed light on the potential applications of our results in algebraic geometry.

In this paper, based on Elkik’s and García’s ideas, we construct Deligne pairing for equidimensional morphisms and prove its functorial properties, which correspond to the functorial properties of Deligne pairing for flat morphisms. Therefore, Our result is a natural generalization of Grothendieck’s second norm functor and counterpart of Deligne pairing for flat morphisms.

Now we briefly introduce our method and innovation.

Definition 1.3
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a morphism of noetherian schemes. We say that
f
is
of finite Tor-dimension
if there exists an integer
d_{0}
such that for each integer
d>d_{0}
, every point
x\in X
, and every
\mathcal{O}_{S,f(x)}
-module
M
, we have
\Tor_{d}^{\mathcal{O}_{S,f(x)}}(\mathcal{O}_{X,x},M)=0
.

Flat morphisms are of finite Tor-dimension. If
S
is regular, then any morphism
f\colon X\to S
is of finite Tor-dimension.

Definition 1.4
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a projective morphism of noetherian schemes. An invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules
\mathcal{L}
is called
f
-
sufficiently ample
if
\mathcal{L}
is
f
-ample, and there exists a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{F}
and a surjective
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module homomorphism
\varphi\colon f^{*}\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{L}
. We say that
(\varphi,\mathcal{F})
is a
presentation
of
\mathcal{L}
, or
\mathcal{F}
generates
\mathcal{L}
.

Locally, every invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module is the difference of two
f
-sufficiently ample invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules, and thus we can reduce the construction of Deligne pairing for general invertible sheaves to that for sufficiently ample invertible sheaves. Then we use Elkik’s and García’s ideas. First we use the presentations to construct a projective space bundle
\mathbf{P}
over
S
and canonical sections of some invertible sheaves on
X\times_{S}\mathbf{P}
. Then use these canonical sections to construct an invertible sheaf
\mathcal{N}
on some open subset of
\mathbf{P}
. After that, we can apply a descent technique to descend
\mathcal{N}
to an invertible sheaf
\mathcal{J}
on
S
, which we will prove to be the Deligne pairing.

A crucial point of this method is to prove that
\mathcal{J}
is independent of the choice of presentations. For this goal, Elkik’s and García’s original ideas are invalid since they use the restriction of norm functor for flat finite morphisms or finite morphisms of finite Tor-dimension on a closed subscheme, while its counterpart in our case, norm functor for finite morphisms over normal schemes, behaves awful when restricting to an arbitrary closed subscheme. To solve this problem, we use algebraic Hartogs’ theorem. We can assume that
S
is integral. Over the generic point
\eta
or the local ring
A=\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,P}
of a point
P
of codimension 1 in
S
, the morphisms
f_{\eta}\colon X\to\eta
and
f_{A}\colon X_{A}\to\Spec A
are both of finite Tor-dimension. Thus the stalks
\mathcal{J}_{\eta}
and
\mathcal{J}_{P}
, viewing as invertible sheaves on
\eta
and
\Spec A
respectively, are exactly the Deligne pairings for morphisms of finite Tor-dimension constructed by García in
[
Gar00
]
§2. Then by García’s results in
[
Gar00
]
, for different presentations, there are unique isomorphisms
\psi_{\eta}
and
\psi_{P}
identifying different
\mathcal{J}_{\eta}
’s and
\mathcal{J}_{P}
’s respectively, and the restriction of
\psi_{P}
on
\eta
is exactly
\psi_{\eta}
. Therefore, by algebraic Hartogs’ theorem,
\psi_{\eta}
restricts to a unique isomorphism
\psi
, which identifies different
\mathcal{J}
’s for different presentations. This trick allows us to glue
\mathcal{J}
’s on different open subsets and finally get the Deligne pairing.

One application of our results is the construction of the canonical section of Deligne pairing using sections with purely geometric conditions. This gives a clear explanation for some notations in
[
Zha96
]
and
[
Mor99
]
.

Theorem 1.5

(Theorem
6.7
)
.

Let
X
and
S
be quasi-projective schemes over a field of characteristic 0, and let
X\to S
be a surjective projective morphism of pure relative dimension
d
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}
be invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules, and for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant d+1
, let
s_{i}
be a global section of
\mathcal{L}_{i}
. Suppose that
S
is normal, and that for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
,
Z(s_{1})\cap Z(s_{2})\cdots\cap Z(s_{i})\to S
is of pure relative dimension
d-i
. Then we can construct a canonical global section of
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
, which we denote by
\langle s_{1},\ldots,s_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
. Moreover, if
\bigcap_{i=1}^{d+1}Z(s_{i})=\emptyset
, then
\langle s_{1},\ldots,s_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
is a regular section of
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
.

1.2.
Deligne pairing of hermitian line bundles

For quasi-projective schemes over
\mathbb{C}
and hermitian line bundles on
X
, i.e. invertible sheaves endowed with smooth metrics, we prove that there is a canonical smooth metric on their Deligne pairing, and the metric is compatible with functorial properties. The results are as follows.

Theorem 1.6

(Theorem
8.5
)
.

Let
X
and
S
be quasi-projective schemes over
\mathbb{C}
, and let
f\colon X\to S
be a surjective projective morphism over
\mathbb{C}
of pure relative dimension
d
. Suppose that
S
is normal. Let
(\mathcal{L}_{1},\|\cdot\|_{1}),(\mathcal{L}_{2},\|\cdot\|_{2}),\ldots,(\mathcal{L}_{d+1},\|\cdot\|_{d+1})
be hermitian line bundles on
X
. Then we can endow
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
with a unique smooth metric
\|\cdot\|
such that

(1)

c_{1}(\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S},\|\cdot\|)=\int_{X/S}\prod_{i=1}^{d+1}c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{i},\|\cdot\|_{i}).

(2) Let
d\geqslant 1
, and let
s_{1}
be an
f
-regular section of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
. Then

\log\|[s_{1}]_{X/S}\|=-\int_{X/S}\log\|s_{1}\|_{1}\prod_{i=2}^{d+1}c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{i},\|\cdot\|_{i}).

(3) If
d=0
, then

\log\|\textnormal{Nm}_{X/S}(s)\|=\int_{X/S}\log\|s\|_{1}

for each nonzero rational section
s
of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
.

(4) The natural isomorphisms
\Sigma
’s and
\iota
’s defined in (
4.11
) and (
4.13
) are isometries.

(5) For each good base change (in the sense of Definition
5.2
)
g\colon S^{\prime}\to S
, the isomorphism

\langle g^{\prime*}\mathcal{L}_{1},g^{\prime*}\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,g^{\prime*}\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}}\to g^{*}\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}.

is an isometry, where
X^{\prime}=X\times_{S}S^{\prime}
,
g^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\to X
, and for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant d+1
, the metric on
g^{\prime*}\mathcal{L}_{i}
is
g^{\prime*}\|\cdot\|_{i}
.

Theorem 1.7

(Theorem
8.9
)
.

Let
X
and
S
be quasi-projective schemes over
\mathbb{C}
, and let
f\colon X\to S
be a surjective projective morphism over
\mathbb{C}
of pure relative dimension
d
. Suppose that
S
is normal. Let
(\mathcal{L}_{1},\|\cdot\|_{1}),(\mathcal{L}_{2},\|\cdot\|_{2}),\ldots,(\mathcal{L}_{d+1},\|\cdot\|_{d+1}),(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{1}),(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{2}),\ldots,(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{d+1},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{d+1})
be invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules endowed with smooth metrics. For each
1\leqslant i\leqslant d+1
, let
u_{i}\colon\mathcal{L}_{i}\to\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i}
be an isometry. If we endow
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
and
\langle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
with the canonical metrics, then the following isomorphism of invertible
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules

\langle u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}\colon\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}\to\langle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}

is also an isometry.

Theorem 1.8

(Theorem
8.10
)
.

Let
X
and
S
be quasi-projective schemes over
\mathbb{C}
, and let
f\colon X\to S
be a surjective projective morphism over
\mathbb{C}
of pure relative dimension
d
. Suppose that
S
is integral and normal. Let
(\mathcal{L}_{1},\|\cdot\|_{1}),(\mathcal{L}_{2},\|\cdot\|_{2}),\ldots,(\mathcal{L}_{d},\|\cdot\|_{d})
be invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules endowed with smooth metrics, and let
(\mathcal{M},\|\cdot\|)
be an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module endowed with smooth metrics. We endow
f^{*}\mathcal{M}
with the smooth metric
f^{*}\|\cdot\|
. Let
\delta
be the intersection number of
\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d}
on the generic fiber of
f
(if
d=0
, then let
\delta
be the degree of finite morphism
f
). If we endow
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,f^{*}\mathcal{M},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d}\rangle_{X/S}
with the canonical metric, then the natural isomorphism

\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,f^{*}\mathcal{M},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d}\rangle_{X/S}\to\mathcal{M}^{\otimes\delta}

is an isometry.

The problem of constructing a canonical metric for Deligne pairing of hermitian line bundles is also raised by Deligne in
[
Del87
]
, where he constructed the metric for flat projective morphisms of pure relative dimension 1. For general
d
, Elkik
[
Elk90
]
constructed the metric for Cohen–Macaulay smooth projective morphisms, and Yuan
[
YZ24
]
constructed the metric for flat projective morphisms. Our theorems may be the first result on the canonical metric on Deligne pairing of hermitian line bundles for equidimensional morphisms.

We hope for a similar result over non-archimedean fields and applications of our results in number theory, arithmetic geometry, and algebraic dynamics.

1.3.
Structure of this article

In the second section, we review the construction of norm functor for finite morphisms over normal schemes. Although we basically follow
[
GD61
]
§6.5, we drop the noetherian assumption to prepare for the potential construction of Deligne pairing for equidimensional morphisms over non-noetherian schemes, resembling that for flat morphisms in
[
EF24
]
.

In the third section, we prove a gluing result of different norm functors for a surjective projective morphism of pure relative dimension 1 over a noetherian normal scheme. Results in relative dimensions 0 and 1 are fundamental tools for our construction of Deligne pairing.

In the forth section, we use Elkik’s and García’s ideas to construct Deligne pairing for sufficiently ample invertible sheaves, and then extend the functor to all invertible sheaves by linearity. As we mentioned above, we first use the presentations of sufficiently ample invertible sheaves to construct a projective space bundle
\mathbf{P}
over
S
and canonical sections of some invertible sheaves on
X\times_{S}\mathbf{P}
. Then we use these canonical sections to construct an invertible sheaf
\mathcal{N}
on some open subset of
\mathbf{P}
. After that, we use a descent technique to descend
\mathcal{N}
to an invertible sheaf
\mathcal{J}
on
S
. Finally, we use algebraic Hartogs’ theorem to show that
\mathcal{J}
is independent of the choice of the presentations. More precisely, for different choices, there is a unique isomorphism between them. This uniqueness allows us to glue
\mathcal{J}
’s on different open subsets.

In the fifth section, following the framework of Garcia
[
Gar00
]
, we prove functorial properties of Deligne pairing. These functorial properties correspond to their counterparts of Deligne pairing for flat morphisms. We will see that the algebraic Hartogs’ theorem and Garcia’s results
[
Gar00
]
play an important role in our proof, and such treatment will be used repeatedly in this paper.

For a flat morphism
X\to S
, if we have a suitable sequence
s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{d+1}
of sections of the invertible sheaves
\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}
, then we can define a canonical section of
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
, which is usually denoted by
\langle s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
. Such section is important for analyzing positivity properties of Deligne pairing, and has many other important applications. In the sixth section, we prove similar results for Deligne pairing for equidimensional morphisms and construct the canonical section from suitable section sequences.

In the seventh section, we construct isomorphisms between Deligne pairings induced by isomorphisms between original invertible sheaves, and show that these isomorphisms are compatible with the functorial properties of Deligne pairing. Therefore, we prove that Deligne pairing is a symmetric multi-additive functor between Picard categories.

In the eighth section, we construct a canonical metric for Deligne pairing of hermitian line bundles for equidimensional morphisms. The basic idea, due to Elkik
[
Elk90
]
, is taking an
f
-regular section and then using induction on the relative dimension
d
.

2.
Norm functor

In this section, we recall some basic facts on the norm functor for finite morphisms over normal schemes.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
X
be a scheme such that every point of
X
has a neighborhood which meets only finitely many irreducible components of
X
. Suppose that for each
x\in X
, the nilradical of
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
is a prime ideal. Then the irreducible components
X
are disjoint, and each irreducible component is open in
X
.

Proof.

Let
x\in X
and
U=\Spec A
be an affine open neighborhood of
x
. Suppose that
x
corresponds to prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}_{x}\subseteq A
. We know that each minimal prime of
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
corresponds to a minimal prime of
A
contained in
\mathfrak{p}_{x}
, and then corresponds to an irreducible component of
U
containing
x
. Since the nilradical of
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
is prime, by the above correspondence we deduce that every
x\in X
in contained in a unique irreducible component of any affine open neighborhood
U
. Thus the irreducible components of
X
are disjoint.

Let
C
be an irreducible component of
X
, and
x\in C
be any point. Let
U_{x}
be an open neighborhood of
x
which meets only finitely many irreducible components
C=C_{1},C_{2},\ldots,C_{n}
of
X
. Each
C_{i}\bigcap U_{x}
is closed in
U_{x}
, and does not intersect other
C_{j}\bigcap U_{x}\ (j\neq i)
. Then
C\bigcap U_{x}
is open in
U_{x}
, and thus open in
X
. Then we deduce that
C
is open in
X
since
C=\bigcup_{x\in C}(C\bigcap U_{x})
.
∎

Definition 2.2
.

Let
X
be a scheme. An
quasi-coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra of finite type
on
X
is a quasi-coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module of finite type (in the sense of
[
GD60
]
Chapter 0 §5) such that for each open subset
U\subseteq X
,
\mathcal{A}(U)
is an
\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)
-algebra, and the algebra structure is compatible with restriction morphisms.

For simplicity, we introduce the following assumption:

(*)
X
is a normal scheme such that every point of
X
has a neighborhood which meets only finitely many irreducible components of
X
, and
\mathcal{A}
is a quasi-coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra of finite type on
X
.

For a scheme
X
, if
X
and
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
satisfy
(*)
, we will simply say that
X
satisfies
(*)
.

For example, if
X
is a normal integral scheme or a normal locally noetherian scheme, then
X
satisfies
(*)
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Suppose that
X
and
\mathcal{A}
satisfy
(*)
. Then there is a morphism
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}
which satisfies the following properties:

(1) for each open subset
U\subseteq X
and
f,g\in\mathcal{A}(U)
,

\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(fg)=\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(f)\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(g);

(2) for each open subset
U\subseteq X

\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(1)=1;

(3) for each connected open subset
U\subseteq X
and
s\in\mathcal{O}(U)
,

\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(s\cdot 1)=s^{n},

where
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
and only depends on the connected component of
X
that
U
lies in.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.1
, we know that the irreducible components of
X
are disjoint, and each irreducible component is open in
X
. Thus sheaves on
X
split into sheaves on each irreducible components of
X
. Moreover, each irreducible component
X
is also a connected component of
X
. Thus we only need to construct
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}
on each irreducible component of
X
. Therefore, we can assume that
X
is a normal integral scheme and
\mathcal{A}
is a quasi-coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra of finite type. It remains to construct a morphism
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}
which satisfies (1), (2), and

(3)^{\prime}
for each open subset
U\subseteq X
and
s\in\mathcal{O}(U)
,

\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(s\cdot 1)=s^{n},

where
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
is a constant.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{X}
be the sheaf of rational functions on
X
. Then
\mathcal{R}_{X}
is a constant sheaf, and the restriction morphisms are identity. For each nonempty open subset
U\subseteq X
,
\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)
is an integral domain,
\mathcal{R}_{X}(U)
is the field of fraction of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)
, and
\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)
is integrally closed in
\mathcal{R}_{X}(U)
. Since
\mathcal{A}
is quasi-coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module of finite type, by
[
GD60
]
Chapter 1 (7.3.6),
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{R}_{X}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{R}_{X}^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
.

For each nonempty open subset,
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{R}_{X}(U)
is a
n
-dimensional
\mathcal{R}_{X}(U)
-vector space, and each
f\in\mathcal{A}(U)
defines a
\mathcal{R}_{X}(U)
-linear homomorphism

L_{f}\colon\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{R}_{X}(U)\to\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{R}_{X}(U),\quad v\mapsto fv.

Then we define

\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(f)=\det(L_{f}).

Since
\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)
is integrally closed, by
[
GD60
]
Chapter 1 (6.4.3),
\det(L_{f})\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)
. Thus
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}
sends
\mathcal{A}(U)
to
\mathcal{O}_{X}(U)
. Since
\mathcal{A}
is an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra,
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}
defines a morphism
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}
. And (1), (2),
(3)^{\prime}
can be easily deduced from the properties of determinants of linear homomorphisms. Notes that the
n
in
(3)^{\prime}
is exactly the rank of
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{R}_{X}
as an
\mathcal{R}_{X}
-module.
∎

For a general ringed space
(X,\mathcal{A})
, let
\Inv(X,\mathcal{A})
denote the category of invertible
\mathcal{A}
-modules (defined as locally free
\mathcal{A}
-sheaves of rank 1, following
[
GD60
]
Chapter 1 §5.4) with morphisms being
\mathcal{A}
-module homomorphisms. We avoid using the word “Picard” since Picard groups and categories usually only concern isomorphisms between invertible sheaves. If
X
is a scheme, we abbreviate
\Inv(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
to
\Inv(X)
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{A})
be a ringed space,
\mathcal{L}
be an invertible
\mathcal{A}
-module, and
s\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal{L})
be a global section. We say that
s
is
\mathcal{A}
-
regular
if the morphism
\mathcal{A}_{X}\xrightarrow{\cdot s}\mathcal{L}
, multiplying by
s
, is injective. If
X
is a scheme and
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
, we will simply say that
s
is
regular
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Suppose that
X
and
\mathcal{A}
satisfy
(*)
. Then we can associate each invertible
\mathcal{A}
-module
\mathcal{L}
with an invertible
\mathcal{A}
-module
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{L})
such that

(1)
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{O}_{X}
, which shows that our abuse of notation is reasonable;

(2) for two invertible
\mathcal{A}
-modules
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
,
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{L}_{1}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{L}_{2})
is canonically isomorphic to
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{L}_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{L}_{2})
;

(3) for each homomorphism
h\colon\mathcal{L}_{1}\to\mathcal{L}_{2}
of invertible
\mathcal{A}
-modules, we have a functorial homomorphism
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(h)\colon\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{L}_{1})\to\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{L}_{2})
of invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules;

(4) if
h
in (3) is an isomorphism, then
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(h)
is also an isomorphism;

(5) if
h
in (4) is injective, then
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(h)
is also injective. In particular, we can associate each
\mathcal{A}
-regular section
s
of an invertible
\mathcal{A}
-module
\mathcal{L}
with a regular section
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(s)
of
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{L})
.

In conclusion,
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}
is a covariant functor from
\Inv(X,\mathcal{A})
to
\Inv(X)
, and preserves tensor products and injective homomorphisms.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.1
, we can assume that
X
is a normal integral scheme.

(1), (2), (3), and (4) can be directly deduced from the arguments in
[
GD61
]
(6.5.2) and (6.5.3). Although
X
is assumed to be normal and locally noetherian in
[
GD61
]
(6.5.2) and (6.5.3), the only application of this condition is to ensure that
\mathcal{R}_{X}
—the sheaf of rational functions on
X
—is a quasi-coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module. And this can also deduced from our assumption (*) by
[
GD60
]
Chapter 1 (7.3.3). Thus the arguments in
[
GD61
]
(6.5.2) and (6.5.3) work under our conditions.

To prove (5), it suffices to check on small enough affine open subsets. Suppose that
X=\Spec A
,
\Gamma(X,\mathcal{A})=B
, and
\mathcal{L}_{1}=\mathcal{L}_{2}=\mathcal{A}
, where
A
is an integrally closed domain, and
B
is a finite
A
-algebra (in the sense of
[
AM18
]
Chapter 2). Then
h
corresponds to an injective
B
-module endomorphism
u\colon B\to B
. Let
f=u(1)
. Then
\textnormal{Nm}_{B/A}(u)\colon A\to A
is multiplying by
\textnormal{Nm}_{B/A}(f)
. Note that
\textnormal{Nm}_{B/A}(f)\in A
. Let
K
be the field of fractions of
A
. Then
\textnormal{Nm}_{B/A}(f)=\det(u\otimes_{A}\id_{K}\colon B\otimes_{A}K\to B\otimes_{A}K)
. Since
K
is the localization of
A
at the prime ideal (0),
u\otimes_{A}\id_{K}
is an injective
K
-vector space homomorphism, and thus
\textnormal{Nm}_{B/A}(f)\neq 0
. Therefore,
\textnormal{Nm}_{B/A}(u)
is injective, i.e.
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(h)
is injective.
∎

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
X
be a normal integral scheme and
\mathcal{A}
be a quasi-coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra of finite type on
X
. Then there exists an integer
n
such that for each invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{L}
,
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{L}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{A})
is canonically isomorphic to
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes n}
.

Proof.

Directly implied by Proposition
2.3
(3).
∎

Theorem 2.7
.

Suppose that
X
satisfies
(*)
, and let
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be a finite morphism of schemes. Then we have a covariant functor

\textnormal{Nm}_{f}\colon\Inv(X^{\prime})\to\Inv(X),

which preserves tensor products and injective homomorphisms.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}=f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
. Since
f
is a finite morphism,
\mathcal{A}
is a quasi-coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-algebra of finite type. Moreover, for each invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}
-module
\mathcal{L}
on
X^{\prime}
, by
[
GD61
]
(6.1.2) we know that
f_{*}\mathcal{L}
is an invertible
\mathcal{A}
-module on
X
. Thus
f_{*}
induces a covariant functor from
\Inv(X^{\prime})
to
\Inv(X,\mathcal{A})
. It is obvious that
f_{*}
preserves tensor products and injective homomorphisms. We define
\textnormal{Nm}_{f}
to be the composition of
f_{*}
and
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}
, i.e.

\textnormal{Nm}_{f}=\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}\circ f_{*}\colon\Inv(X^{\prime})\to\Inv(X),\quad\mathcal{L}\mapsto\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}(f_{*}\mathcal{L}).

Since both
f_{*}
and
\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathcal{A}}
preserves tensor products and injective homomorphisms, so does
\textnormal{Nm}_{f}
.
∎

Sometimes we also use
\textnormal{Nm}_{X^{\prime}/X}
to denote
\textnormal{Nm}_{f}
.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
X
be a normal integral scheme, and
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be a finite morphism of schemes. Then there exists an integer
n
such that for each invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{L}
,
\textnormal{Nm}_{f}(f^{*}\mathcal{L})=\mathcal{L}^{\otimes n}
.

Proof.

Directly implied by Proposition
2.6
.
∎

Proposition 2.9

(
[
GD61
]
(6.5.8))
.

Suppose that
X
and
Y
satisfy
(*)
. Let
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be a finite morphism, and
g\colon Y\to X
be a morphism of schemes such that
g
restricting to each irreducible component of
Y
is a dominant morphism to an irreducible component of
X
. Let
Y^{\prime}=X^{\prime}\times_{X}Y
, and consider the Cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Then for each invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}
-module
\mathcal{L}
,

\textnormal{Nm}_{f^{\prime}}(g^{\prime*}\mathcal{L})=g^{*}\textnormal{Nm}_{f}(\mathcal{L}).

Proof.

The noetherian assumption in
[
GD61
]
(6.5.8) only used for the definition of the norm functor. And its proof of such an isomorphism is applicable here.
∎

3.
Norm functor in relative dimension 1 case

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
A
be a noetherian integrally closed domain, let
M,M^{\prime}
be finite
A
-modules, and let
u,u^{\prime}
be
A
-module endomorphisms of
M,M^{\prime}
respectively. If
\coker(u)\cong\coker(u^{\prime})
as
A
-modules, then there exists a unit
a\in A
such that
\det(u)=a\det(u^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Let
K
be the field of fractions of
A
, and let
M_{\textnormal{tor}},M^{\prime}_{\textnormal{tor}}
be the torsion submodules of
M,M^{\prime}
respectively. Since
M\otimes_{A}K
is canonically isomorphic to
(M/M_{\textnormal{tor}})\otimes_{A}K
, if we replace
M,M^{\prime}
by
M/M_{\textnormal{tor}},M^{\prime}/M^{\prime}_{\textnormal{tor}}
respectively, the conditions and conclusions of the lemma does not change. Thus we can assume that
M
and
M^{\prime}
are torsionfree.

Moreover, we can assume that
u
and
u^{\prime}
are injective. Let
u_{K}=u\otimes_{A}\id_{K}
and
u^{\prime}_{K}=u^{\prime}\otimes_{A}\id_{K}
. Then
\coker(u_{K})=\coker(u)\otimes_{A}K\cong\coker(u^{\prime})\otimes_{A}K=\coker(u^{\prime}_{K})
. Thus if one of
u
and
u^{\prime}
is not injective, so is the other, and then
\det(u)=\det(u^{\prime})=0
. In this case, we can take
a=1
.

We consider the case that
A
is a PID. Since
M
and
M^{\prime}
are torsionfree, they are free
A
-modules. Since
u
and
u^{\prime}
are injective, both
\coker(u)
and
\coker(u^{\prime})
are torsion. Let
(q_{1}),(q_{2}),\ldots,(q_{r})
be the invariants of
\coker(u)
and
\coker(u^{\prime})
. Then both
\det(u)
and
\det(u^{\prime})
differ from
q_{1}q_{2}\ldots q_{r}
by a unit in
A
. Thus there exists a unit
a\in A
such that
\det(u)=a\det(u^{\prime})
.

Now we consider the general case. Since
u
and
u^{\prime}
are injective, both
\det(u)
and
\det(u^{\prime})
are nonzero. So there exists
a,b\in K
such that
\det(u)=a\det(u^{\prime})
and
\det(u^{\prime})=b\det(u)
. It suffices to prove that
a,b\in A
. Since
A
is integrally closed,

A=\bigcap_{\text{height}\,\mathfrak{p}=1}A_{\mathfrak{p}},

where the intersection is taken over all prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}\subseteq A
of height 1, we only need to prove that
a,b\in A_{\mathfrak{p}}
for all prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}\subseteq A
of height 1. Let
u_{\mathfrak{p}}=u\otimes_{A}\id_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}
and
u^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}=u^{\prime}\otimes_{A}\id_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}}
. Then
u_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
u^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}
are injective, and
\coker(u_{\mathfrak{p}})=\coker(u)\otimes_{A}A_{\mathfrak{p}}\cong\coker(u^{\prime})\otimes_{A}A_{\mathfrak{p}}=\coker(u^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}})
. Since
M_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{A}K=M\otimes_{A}K
and
M^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{A}K=M^{\prime}\otimes_{A}K
, we have
\det(u_{\mathfrak{p}})=\det(u)
and
\det(u^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{p}})=\det(u^{\prime})
. Since
A
is integrally closed and the height of
\mathfrak{p}
is 1,
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a PID. Therefore,
a,b\in A_{\mathfrak{p}}
.
∎

Let
X
be a scheme, let
\mathcal{L}
be an invertible sheaf on
X
, and let
s\colon\mathcal{O}_{X}\to\mathcal{L}
be a global section of
\mathcal{L}
. We denote by
Z(s)
the closed subscheme defined by
s
, or more precisely, the closed subscheme defined by ideal sheaf
\im(s^{\vee}\colon\mathcal{L}^{\vee}\to\mathcal{O}_{X})
. Locally, if we identify
\mathcal{L}
with
\mathcal{O}_{X}
, then the ideal sheaf of
Z(s)
is
s\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a finite morphism, and let
\mathcal{L}
be an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module. Then for every point
t\in S
, there exists an open neighborhood
U
of
t
such that
\mathcal{L}|_{f^{-1}(U)}\cong\mathcal{O}_{f^{-1}(U)}
.

Proof.

By
[
GD61
]
Chapter II (6.1.12.1), there is an open neighborhood
V
of
f^{-1}(t)
such that
\mathcal{L}_{V}\cong\mathcal{O}_{V}
. Let
Z=X\setminus V
and
U=S\setminus f(Z)
. Then
Z
is a closed subset of
X
, and thus
U
is an open neighborhood of
t
. Obviously,
U\subseteq f(V)
, and thus
f^{-1}(U)\subseteq V
. Therefore,
\mathcal{L}|_{f^{-1}(U)}\cong\mathcal{O}_{f^{-1}(U)}
.
∎

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
X,S
be schemes, and suppose that
S
is normal and locally noetherian. Let
f\colon X\to S
be a surjective projective morphism of pure relative dimension
1
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
be two invertible sheaves on
X
, and let
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
be regular sections of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
respectively. Let
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
be the subscheme defined by
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
respectively. Suppose that both
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
are finite over
S
, and that both
s_{1}|_{Z_{2}}
and
s_{2}|_{Z_{1}}
are regular. Then there exists a unique isomorphism

\alpha\colon\textnormal{Nm}_{Z_{1}/S}(\mathcal{L}_{2}|_{Z_{1}})\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Nm}_{Z_{2}/S}(\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{Z_{2}})

such that

\alpha(\textnormal{Nm}_{Z_{1}/S}(s_{2}|_{Z_{1}}))=\textnormal{Nm}_{Z_{2}/S}(s_{1}|_{Z_{2}}).

Proof.

Since
S
satisfy
(*)
, we can assume that
S
is an integral scheme by Lemma
2.1
.

By Proposition
2.7
,
\textnormal{Nm}_{Z_{1}/S}(s_{2}|_{Z_{1}})
and
\textnormal{Nm}_{Z_{2}/S}(s_{1}|_{Z_{2}})
are regular sections of
\textnormal{Nm}_{Z_{1}/S}(\mathcal{L}_{2}|_{Z_{1}})
and
\textnormal{Nm}_{Z_{2}/S}(\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{Z_{2}})
respectively. By looking at the generic fiber, we deduce that
\alpha
is unique. By uniqueness and gluing, we only need to construct
\alpha
on small enough affine open subsets.

By Lemma
3.2
, we can assume that
S=\Spec A
,
\mathcal{L}_{1}=\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}}
, and that
\mathcal{L}_{2}=\mathcal{O}_{Z_{2}}
. Then
\textnormal{Nm}_{Z_{2}/S}(\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{Z_{2}})=\textnormal{Nm}_{Z_{1}/S}(\mathcal{L}_{2}|_{Z_{1}})=\mathcal{O}_{S}
. Let
\mathscr{I}_{1}
and
\mathscr{I}_{2}
be the ideal sheaves defined by
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
respectively. We view
s_{1}|_{Z_{2}}
and
s_{2}|_{Z_{1}}
as morphisms

s_{1}|_{Z_{2}}\colon\mathcal{O}_{Z_{2}}\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad s_{2}|_{Z_{1}}\colon\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}}\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}}

respectively. Then

\coker s_{2}|_{Z_{1}}=\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathscr{I}_{2}=\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Z_{2}}\\
=\mathcal{O}_{Z_{2}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}}=\mathcal{O}_{Z_{2}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathscr{I}_{1}=\coker s_{1}|_{Z_{2}}.

By Lemma
3.1
, there exists a unit
a\in A
such that
\det(s_{2}|_{Z_{1}})=a\det(s_{1}|_{Z_{2}})
. Then we can take
\alpha
to be multiplying by
a^{-1}
.
∎

4.
Deligne pairing for equidimensional morphisms

4.1.
Preliminary

Let
n
be a positive integer, and let
\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}
be invertible sheaves on
X
. For each
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, let
s_{i}\colon\mathcal{O}_{X}\to\mathcal{L}_{i}
be a global section of
\mathcal{L}_{i}
. Let
Z(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}Z(s_{i})
. Locally, if we identify all
\mathcal{L}_{i}
’s with
\mathcal{O}_{X}
, then the ideal sheaf of
Z(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
is
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
X
,
\mathcal{L}_{i}
’s, and
s_{i}
’s be as above. For
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, abbreviate
Z(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
to
Z_{i}
. We say that
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
is a
regular sequence
if
s_{1}
is a regular section of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and for each
2\leqslant i\leqslant n
,
s_{i}|_{Z_{i-1}}
is a regular section of
\mathcal{L}_{i}|_{Z_{i-1}}
.

Note that our definition of regular sections here is identical to that in Definition
2.4
.

Locally, if
X=\Spec A
and we identify all
\mathcal{L}_{i}
’s with
\mathcal{O}_{X}
, then
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
is a regular sequence means that
s_{1}
is not a zero-divisor of
A
and for each
2\leqslant i\leqslant n
,
s_{i}
is not a zero-divisor of
A/(s_{1},\ldots,s_{i-1})
, i.e.
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
is an
A
-sequence in the sense of
[
Ser00
]
Chapter IV §A.4.

Proposition 4.2
.

Whether the sequence
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
is regular is independent of its order.

Proof.

Refer to
[
Ser00
]
Chapter IV §A.4 p.60.
∎

4.2.
Deligne pairing of sufficiently ample invertible sheaves

Let
X\to S
be a projective morphism of noetherian schemes, let
\mathcal{L}
be an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module, let
\mathcal{F}
be a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module, and let
\varphi\colon f^{*}\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{L}
a surjective
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module homomorphism. Let
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{F}^{\vee})
and
\xi=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{P}}(1)
. Consider the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathbf{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathbf{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

We have a natural surjective morphism
\pi^{*}\mathcal{F}^{\vee}\to\xi
, which induce a natural morphism
f_{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(\xi^{\vee})\to f_{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(\pi^{*}\mathcal{F})=\pi_{X}^{*}(f^{*}\mathcal{F})
. Composing with
\pi_{X}^{*}(\varphi)\colon\pi_{X}^{*}(f^{*}\mathcal{F})\to\pi_{X}^{*}(\mathcal{L})
, we get a morphism
f_{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(\xi^{\vee})\to\pi_{X}^{*}(\mathcal{L})
. Tensoring with
f_{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(\xi)
, we get a morphism

(4.1)

\sigma\colon\mathcal{O}_{X_{\mathbf{P}}}=f_{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(\xi^{\vee})\otimes f_{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(\xi)\to\pi_{X}^{*}(\mathcal{L})\otimes f_{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(\xi).

We identify
\sigma
with a section of
\mathcal{L}\boxtimes\xi\coloneq\pi_{X}^{*}(\mathcal{L})\otimes f_{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(\xi)
.

Proposition 4.3

(García)
.

Let
\mathcal{L},\xi,\sigma
be as above. Then
\sigma
is a regular section of
\mathcal{L}\boxtimes\xi
.

Proof.

Refer to
[
Gar00
]
§2 2.2.
∎

Now let
f\colon X\to S
be a surjective projective morphism of noetherian schemes of pure relative dimension
d
. Let
n
be an integer between
1
and
d
, and let
\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}
be invertible sheaves on
S
such that for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, there exists a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{F}_{i}
and a surjection
\varphi_{i}\colon f^{*}\mathcal{F}_{i}\to\mathcal{L}_{i}
. For each
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, let
\mathbf{P}_{i}=\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{F}^{\vee}_{i})
,
\mathbf{P}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{P}_{i}
,
\xi_{i}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{P}_{i}}(1)
, and let
\sigma_{i}
be the regular section of
\mathcal{L}_{i}\boxtimes\xi_{i}
as above. We may view
\sigma_{i}
as a section of
\mathcal{L}_{i}\boxtimes\xi_{i}
on
X_{\mathbf{P}}=X\times_{S}\mathbf{P}
by base change. Let
Z_{i}=Z(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{i})=\bigcap_{j=1}^{i}Z(\sigma_{j})
be the closed subscheme of
X_{\mathbf{P}}
.

Proposition 4.4

(García)
.

\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{n}
is a regular sequence of
\mathcal{L}_{1}\boxtimes\xi_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}\boxtimes\xi_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}\boxtimes\xi_{n}
on
X_{\mathbf{P}}
.

Proof.

Refer to
[
Gar00
]
Proposition 2.3.1.
∎

Now let
f\colon X\to S
,
\mathcal{F}_{i}
,
\varphi_{i}
be as above, while
\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n}
are
f
-sufficiently ample invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules. By
[
Sta23
]
Lemma 29.29.2, we know that
f_{\mathbf{P}}\colon X_{\mathbf{P}}\to\mathbf{P}
is also of pure relative dimension
d
. For each
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, Let

\mathcal{V}_{i}=\{x\in\mathbf{P}\mid f_{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}(x)\cap Z_{i}\text{ is of pure dimension }d-i\}.

Proposition 4.5
.

\mathcal{V}_{i}
is an open subset of
\mathbf{P}
.

Proof.

Let
x\in\mathbf{P}
, and let
k(x)
be its residue field. By
[
GD61
]
(4.6.13), we know that for any
i
and subscheme
Z
of
f_{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}(x)
,
\mathcal{L}_{i}|_{Z}
is
k(x)
-ample. By
[
Gro65
]
(5.3.1.3) and (5.1.8), we deduce that each component of
f_{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}(x)\bigcap Z_{i}
has dimension
\geqslant d-i
. Thus

(4.2)

\mathcal{V}_{i}=\{x\in\mathbf{P}\mid\dim f_{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}(x)\cap Z_{i}\leqslant d-i\}.

By semi-continuity (
[
Gro66
]
(13.1.5)), we know that
\mathcal{V}_{i}
is an open subspace of
\mathbf{P}
.
∎

From (
4.2
) we can see that
\mathcal{V}_{1}\supseteq\mathcal{V}_{2}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq\mathcal{V}_{n}
.

Let
\pi\colon\mathbf{P}\to S
be the natural projection.

Proposition 4.6

(García)
.

For each
s\in S
,
\pi^{-1}(s)\setminus\mathcal{V}_{n}
is a closed subset of
\pi^{-1}(s)
of codimension
\geqslant d-n+2
.

Proof.

Refer to
[
Gar00
]
Proposition 2.4.1.(ii).
∎

In particular, for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
,
\mathcal{V}_{i}\neq\emptyset
.

Now let
n=d
,
Z=Z_{d}
, and
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}_{d}
. Then we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{\mathcal{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathcal{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathbf{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathbf{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where the three squares are all pull-back diagrams. We know that
Z_{\mathcal{V}}\to\mathcal{V}
is a projective morphism of noetherian schemes of pure relative dimension
0
. Thus
Z_{\mathcal{V}}\to\mathcal{V}
is a finite morphism.

Now suppose that
S
is normal. Then
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathcal{V}
are also normal. For any invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{L}_{d+1}
, we can define

(4.3)

\mathcal{N}=\textnormal{Nm}_{Z_{\mathcal{V}}/\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{L}_{d+1}|_{Z_{\mathcal{V}}})

by Theorem
2.7
.

We digress a little to discuss a descent technique. Some letters will be used again to indicate how the technique will be applied.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
n
be an integer and let
S
be a noetherian integral scheme. For each
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, let
\mathbf{P}_{i}\to S
be a projective space bundle (in the sense of
[
Har77
]
Chapter 2 §7) over
S
and
\xi_{i}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{P}_{i}}(1)
. Let
\mathbf{P}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{P}_{i}
, let
\pi\colon\mathbf{P}\to S
, and let
\mathcal{V}
be an open subset of
\mathbf{P}
such that for each
s\in S
,
\dim\pi^{-1}(s)-\dim\pi^{-1}(s)\setminus\mathcal{V}\geqslant 2
. We say that an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}
-module
\mathcal{N}

descends to
S
up to
\xi
-torsion
if there exist integers
r_{i}\ (1\leqslant i\leqslant n)
, an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{J}
, and an
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}
-module isomorphism

(4.4)

\lambda\colon\mathcal{N}\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi^{*}\mathcal{J}\mathop{\boxtimes}_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}^{r_{i}}.

Sometimes we will simply say that
\mathcal{N}
descends to
\mathcal{J}
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
\mathbf{P},S,\mathcal{V}
be as in Definition
4.7
. Then

\Hom_{S}(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})=\Hom_{\mathbf{P}}(\pi^{*}\mathcal{J},\pi^{*}\mathcal{J}^{\prime})=\Hom_{\mathcal{V}}(\pi^{*}\mathcal{J},\pi^{*}\mathcal{J}^{\prime}).

Proof.

Refer to
[
Gro67
]
(21.13.2) and (21.13.4).
∎

Proposition 4.9
.

In Definition
4.7
, all
r_{i}
’s are uniquely determined and
\mathcal{J}
is unique up to a unique isomorphism determined by
\lambda
.

Proof.

Let
s\in S
, and let
k(s)
be its residue field. Then

\Pic(\pi^{-1}(s)\cap\mathcal{V})=\Pic(\pi^{-1}(s))=\Pic(\mathbf{P}_{k(s)})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Pic(\mathbf{P}_{i,k(s)})=\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

And we can show that all
r_{i}
’s are uniquely determined by restricting
\mathcal{N}
to a fiber
\pi^{-1}(s)
over a point
s\in S
. The uniqueness of
\mathcal{J}
is induced by Lemma
4.8
.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Suppose that for each
s\in S
,
\dim\pi^{-1}(s)-\dim\pi^{-1}(s)\setminus\mathcal{V}\geqslant 3
. Then every invertible
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}
-module descends to
S
up to
\xi
-torsion.

Proof.

Refer to
[
Gro05
]
Exposé XII Corollary 4.9 or
[
Elk89
]
I.2.6 Lemma (c).
∎

Now we go back to our original problem. We assume that
S
is a noetherian normal integral scheme, and that
\mathcal{L}_{d+1}
in (
4.3
) is also
f
-sufficiently ample.

Proposition 4.11
.

The
\mathcal{N}
defined by (
4.3
) descends to
S
up to
\xi
-torsion.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{P}^{\prime}=\prod_{i=1}^{d-1}\mathbf{P}_{i}
,
Z^{\prime}=Z(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{d-1})\subseteq X_{\mathbf{P}^{\prime}}
,
\pi^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{P}^{\prime}\to S
, and

\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\{x\in\mathbf{P}^{\prime}\mid f_{\mathbf{P}^{\prime}}^{-1}(x)\bigcap Z^{\prime}\text{ is of pure dimension }1\}.

We see that
Z=(Z^{\prime}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{d})\bigcap Z(\sigma_{d})
. Since the dimension of each component of each fiber decreases at most 1 after intersecting with
Z(\sigma_{d})
, we deduce that
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{d}=\mathbf{P}(\pi^{\prime*}(\mathcal{F}_{d})^{\vee}|_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}})
, where
\mathcal{F}_{d}
is the
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module generating
\mathcal{L}_{d}
. By Proposition
4.6
, for each
s\in S
,
\pi^{\prime-1}(s)\setminus\mathcal{V}_{n}
is a closed subset of
\pi^{\prime-1}(s)
of codimension
\geqslant 3
. Then we know that every invertible
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}
-module descends to
S
up to
\xi^{\prime}
-torsion by Lemma
4.10
. It remains to show that every invertible
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}
-module descends to
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
up to
\xi_{d}
-torsion. Let
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{d}
, and
Z^{\prime\prime}=Z^{\prime}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{d}=Z^{\prime}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\times_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}=(Z^{\prime}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{d})\bigcap f_{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}})
. Then we have commutative diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{\mathcal{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathcal{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathbf{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathbf{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime\prime}\bigcap Z(\sigma_{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{\mathcal{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathbf{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathbf{P}^{\prime}}}

Note that all the small squares in the above two diagrams are Cartesian. We see that
Z^{\prime\prime}\to\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}
is a projective morphism of relative dimension 1,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\mathcal{L}_{d}|_{Z^{\prime}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}
is an
f_{\mathbf{P}^{\prime}}
-sufficiently ample invertible sheaf on
Z^{\prime}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}
,
\sigma_{d}
is a regular section of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\boxtimes\xi_{d}
,
Z_{\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}}\subseteq Z^{\prime\prime}
is the zero locus of
\sigma_{d}
, and
\mathcal{V}
is the open subset of
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}
above which
Z_{\mathcal{V}}
is finite. Therefore, it suffices to prove Proposition
4.11
for
d=1
.

Now we prove the
d=1
case. For
i=1,2
, let
(\varphi_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i})
be a presentation of
\mathcal{L}_{i}
,
\mathbf{P}_{i}=\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{F}_{i}^{\vee})
,
\xi_{i}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{P}_{i}}(1)
,
\sigma_{i}
be the natural section defined by (
4.1
),
Z_{i}=Z(\sigma_{i})\subseteq X\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{i}
the zero locus of
\sigma_{i}
,
\mathcal{V}_{i}
be the nonempty open subset of
\mathbf{P}_{i}
above which
Z_{i}
is finite, and let
Z^{\prime}_{i}=Z_{i,\mathcal{V}_{i}}
. Then we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{V}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{V}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{1}.}

Since
S
is normal, both
\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
\mathcal{V}_{2}
are normal. Then we can define

\mathcal{N}_{1}=\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathbf{P}_{1}\times_{S}Z^{\prime}_{2}/\mathbf{P}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{V}_{2}}(\mathcal{L}_{1}\boxtimes\xi_{1})

and

\mathcal{N}_{2}=\textnormal{Nm}_{Z^{\prime}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}/\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}}(\mathcal{L}_{2}\boxtimes\xi_{2}),

with regular sections

s_{1}=\textnormal{Nm}_{\mathbf{P}_{1}\times_{S}Z^{\prime}_{2}/\mathbf{P}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{V}_{2}}(\sigma_{1})\quad\text{and}\quad s_{2}=\textnormal{Nm}_{Z^{\prime}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}/\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}}(\sigma_{2})

respectively, where we view
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
as sections of
\mathcal{L}_{1}\boxtimes\xi_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}\boxtimes\xi_{2}
respectively. Note that the zero locus of
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
on
X\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}
are
Z_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}
and
\mathbf{P}_{1}\times_{S}Z_{2}
respectively. By Proposition
4.4
,
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
is a regular sequence. And then by Proposition
4.2
,
\sigma_{1}|_{\mathbf{P}_{1}\times_{S}Z^{\prime}_{2}}
and
\sigma_{2}|_{Z^{\prime}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}}
are regular sections of

\mathcal{L}_{1}\boxtimes\xi_{1}|_{\mathbf{P}_{1}\times_{S}Z^{\prime}_{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{L}_{2}\boxtimes\xi_{2}|_{Z^{\prime}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}}

respectively. By Proposition
3.3
, identifying
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
, we can glue
\mathcal{N}_{1}
and
\mathcal{N}_{2}
on
\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}\bigcap\mathbf{P}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{V}_{2}=\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{V}_{2}
to get an invertible sheaf
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}\bigcup\mathbf{P}_{1}\times_{S}\mathcal{V}_{2}
. Let

\pi_{1}\colon\mathbf{P}_{1}\to S,\quad\pi_{2}\colon\mathbf{P}_{2}\to S,\quad\pi\colon\mathbf{P}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}\to S.

be the natural projections, and let
F_{1},F_{2},F
be the complements of
\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},\mathcal{V}
in
\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2},\mathbf{P}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}
respectively. By definition,
F=F_{1}\times_{S}F_{2}
. By Proposition
4.6
, for
i=1,2
and each
s\in S
,
F_{i,s}
is a closed subset of
\pi_{i}^{-1}(s)
of codimension
\geqslant 2
. Thus
F_{s}
is a closed subset of
\pi^{-1}(s)
of codimension
\geqslant 4
. Then by Lemma
4.10
,
\mathcal{N}
descends to
S
up to
\xi
-torsion, i.e. there exist integers
r_{1},r_{2}
, and an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{J}
such that

\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\cong\pi^{*}\mathcal{J}\boxtimes\xi_{1}^{r_{1}}\boxtimes\xi_{2}^{r_{2}}

on
\mathcal{V}
. Thus

\mathcal{N}_{2}\cong\pi^{*}\mathcal{J}\boxtimes\xi_{1}^{r_{1}}\boxtimes\xi_{2}^{r_{2}}

on
\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}
. Take a closed point
x
of
\mathbf{P}_{2}
. Then we get a closed immersion
\iota_{x}\colon\mathbf{P}_{1}\to\mathbf{P}_{1}\times\mathbf{P}_{2}
and an isomorphism

(4.5)

\iota_{x}^{*}\,\mathcal{N}_{2}\cong\pi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{J}\boxtimes\xi_{1}^{r_{1}}

on
\mathcal{V}_{1}
.

Since
S
is an integral scheme, so are
\mathbf{P}_{1}
,
\mathcal{V}_{1}
, and
\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}
. Thus by Proposition
2.9
and the following Cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

we have an isomorphism

\textnormal{Nm}_{Z^{\prime}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}/\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}}(\mathcal{L}_{2})=p^{*}\textnormal{Nm}_{Z^{\prime}_{1}/\mathcal{V}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{2}).

Since
p\circ\iota_{x}=\id
, the above equation and (
4.5
) implies that

\displaystyle\textnormal{Nm}_{Z^{\prime}_{1}/\mathcal{V}_{1}}

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{2})\otimes\iota_{x}^{*}\,\textnormal{Nm}_{Z^{\prime}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}/\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}}(p_{1}^{*}\,\xi_{2})

\displaystyle=\iota_{x}^{*}\textnormal{Nm}_{Z^{\prime}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}/\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}}(\mathcal{L}_{2})\otimes\iota_{x}^{*}\,\textnormal{Nm}_{Z^{\prime}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}/\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}}(p_{1}^{*}\,\xi_{2})

\displaystyle=\iota_{x}^{*}\textnormal{Nm}_{Z^{\prime}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}/\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}}(\mathcal{L}_{2}\boxtimes\xi_{2})

(4.6)

\displaystyle=\iota_{x}^{*}\mathcal{N}_{2}\cong\pi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{J}\boxtimes\xi_{1}^{r_{1}}

on
\mathcal{V}_{1}
, where
p_{1}\colon Z^{\prime}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}\to\mathbf{P}_{2}
.

From the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}

and Proposition
2.8
, we deduce that

\iota_{x}^{*}\,\textnormal{Nm}_{Z^{\prime}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}/\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}}(p_{1}^{*}\,\xi_{2})=\iota_{x}^{*}\,\textnormal{Nm}_{Z^{\prime}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}/\mathcal{V}_{1}\times_{S}\mathbf{P}_{2}}(q^{*}p_{2}^{*}\,\xi_{2})\\
=\iota_{x}^{*}(p_{2}^{*}\,\xi_{2})^{\otimes m}=\iota_{x}^{*}p_{2}^{*}(\xi_{2}^{\otimes m})=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}_{1}},

where
m
is an integer.
Thus (
4.6
) becomes

(4.7)

\textnormal{Nm}_{Z^{\prime}_{1}/\mathcal{V}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{2})\cong\pi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{J}\boxtimes\xi_{1}^{r_{1}}

on
\mathcal{V}_{1}
. Therefore,
\textnormal{Nm}_{Z^{\prime}_{1}/\mathcal{V}_{1}}(\mathcal{L}_{2}|_{Z^{\prime}_{1}})
, which is exactly
\mathcal{N}
defined by (
4.3
), descends to
S
up to
\xi_{1}
-torsion.
∎

By Proposition
4.11
, there exists an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{J}
and integers
r_{i}
such that (
4.4
) holds.

Proposition 4.12
.

The
\mathcal{J}
defined by Proposition (
4.11
) is independent of the choice of presentations
(\varphi_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i})\ (1\leqslant i\leqslant d)
of
\mathcal{L}_{i}
’s.

A more precise statement is as follows. For each choice of presentations
\mathbf{V}=((\varphi_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i}))_{1\leqslant i\leqslant d}
, we denote the
\mathcal{J}
defined by Proposition (
4.11
) by
\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}}
. Then for any two choices of presentations
\mathbf{V}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{V}^{\prime\prime}
, we have a canonical isomorphism

\theta_{\mathbf{V},\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}\colon\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}

satisfying
\theta_{\mathbf{V},\mathbf{V}}=\id
and
\theta_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime},\mathbf{V}^{\prime\prime}}\circ\theta_{\mathbf{V},\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}=\theta_{\mathbf{V},\mathbf{V}^{\prime\prime}}
.

Proof.

Let
\eta
be the generic point of
S
. Let
X_{\eta}=X\times_{S}\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,\eta}
,

\mathcal{L}_{i,\eta}=\mathcal{L}_{i}\times_{S}\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,\eta}=\mathcal{L}_{i}|_{X_{\eta}}
,
\mathcal{F}_{i,\eta}=\mathcal{F}_{i}\times_{S}\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,\eta}=\mathcal{F}_{i}|_{\eta}
. We show them in the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{i,\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{i,\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

\varphi_{i,\eta}\colon f_{\eta}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{i,\eta}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{i,\eta},\qquad\varphi_{i}\colon f^{*}\mathcal{F}_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{i}.

We see that for
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
,
(\varphi_{i,\eta},\mathcal{F}_{i,\eta})
is a presentation of
\mathcal{L}_{i,\eta}
. Therefore, we can apply the above operation for

f\colon X\to S,\quad\mathcal{L}_{i},\ (\varphi_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i}),\ (1\leqslant i\leqslant d),\ \mathcal{L}_{d+1}

to

f_{\eta}\colon X_{\eta}\to\eta,\quad\mathcal{L}_{i,\eta},\ (\varphi_{i,\eta},\mathcal{F}_{\eta}),\ (1\leqslant i\leqslant d),\ \mathcal{L}_{d+1,\eta},

and then we can get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{\mathcal{V},\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathcal{V},\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\mathbf{P},\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}_{\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}_{\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathcal{V},\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X,\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathbf{P},\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\eta}}

an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{V_{\eta}}
-module

\mathcal{N}_{\eta}=\textnormal{Nm}_{Z_{\mathcal{V},\eta}/\mathcal{V}_{\eta}}(\mathcal{L}_{d+1,\eta}|_{Z_{\mathcal{V},\eta}}),

and an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{\eta}
-module
\mathcal{J}_{\eta}
which
\mathcal{N}_{\eta}
descends to up to
\xi_{\eta}
torsion.

Since all the operations are commutative with the base change
\eta\to S
, the subscript
\eta
not only indicates the objects are constructed in the
\eta
case, but also means that they are exactly the base change of corresponding objects in the
S
case along
\eta\to S
, i.e.

We see that
\mathcal{N}_{\eta}=\mathcal{N}\times_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{V}_{\eta}
. Then by Proposition
4.9
, we have a canonical isomorphism
\mathcal{J}_{\eta}=\mathcal{J}\times_{S}\eta
. Since
\eta
is a regular scheme,
\mathcal{J}_{\eta}
is exactly the Deligne pairing
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,\eta},\mathcal{L}_{2,\eta},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1,\eta}\rangle_{X_{\eta}/\eta}
constructed in
[
Gar00
]
, and thus is independent of the choice of representation
\mathbf{V}
. For clarification, we use
\psi_{\eta}
to denote the following canonical isomorphism

\psi_{\eta}\colon\mathcal{J}\times_{S}\eta\to\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,\eta},\mathcal{L}_{2,\eta},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1,\eta}\rangle_{X_{\eta}/\eta}.

Let
S^{(1)}
be the set of all codimension 1 points of
S
. Let
P\in S^{(1)}
, and let
A=\mathcal{O}_{S,P}
,
X_{A}=X\times_{S}\Spec A
,

\mathcal{L}_{i,A}=\mathcal{L}_{i}\times_{S}\Spec A=\mathcal{L}_{i}|_{X_{A}}
,
\mathcal{F}_{i,A}=\mathcal{F}_{i}\times_{S}\Spec A=\mathcal{F}_{i}|_{\Spec A}
. Applying the above process for
\eta\to S
to
\Spec A\to S
, we get the following diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{i,A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{i,A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

\varphi_{i,A}\colon f_{A}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{i,A}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{i,A},\qquad\varphi_{i}\colon f^{*}\mathcal{F}_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{i},

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{31.12985pt}{-31.83614pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 43.07 -44.05)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{V_{A}}
-module

\mathcal{N}_{A}=\textnormal{Nm}_{Z_{\mathcal{V},A}/\mathcal{V}_{A}}(\mathcal{L}_{d+1,A}|_{Z_{\mathcal{V},A}}),

an invertible
A
-module
\mathcal{J}_{A}
which
\mathcal{N}_{A}
descends to up to
\xi_{A}
torsion, and a canonical isomorphism
\mathcal{J}_{A}=\mathcal{J}\times_{S}\Spec A
. Since
\Spec A
is a regular scheme,
\mathcal{J}_{A}
is exactly the Deligne pairing
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,A},\mathcal{L}_{2,A},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1,A}\rangle_{X_{A}/\Spec A}
constructed in
[
Gar00
]
, and thus is independent of the choice of representation
\mathbf{V}
. We use
\psi_{P}
to denote the following canonical isomorphism

\psi_{P}\colon\mathcal{J}\times_{S}\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,P}\to\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,A},\mathcal{L}_{2,A},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1,A}\rangle_{X_{A}/\Spec A}.

Proposition
4.9
tells us that the base change of
\psi_{P}
along
\eta\to\Spec A
is exactly the canonical isomorphism
\psi_{\eta}
, and thus we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{J}\times_{S}\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,A},\mathcal{L}_{2,A},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1,A}\rangle_{X_{A}/\Spec A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{J}\times_{S}\eta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,\eta},\mathcal{L}_{2,\eta},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1,\eta}\rangle_{X_{\eta}/\eta}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\eta}}

Now let
\mathbf{V}^{\prime}
be another presentation. Then we get another
\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}
, canonical isomorphisms

\psi^{\prime}_{\eta}\colon\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}\times_{S}\eta\to\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,\eta},\mathcal{L}_{2,\eta},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1,\eta}\rangle_{X_{\eta}/\eta}

and

\psi^{\prime}_{P}\colon\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}\times_{S}\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,P}\to\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,A},\mathcal{L}_{2,A},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1,A}\rangle_{X_{A}/\Spec A}

for each
P\in S^{(1)}
, and a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}\times_{S}\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,A},\mathcal{L}_{2,A},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1,A}\rangle_{X_{A}/\Spec A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}\times_{S}\eta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,\eta},\mathcal{L}_{2,\eta},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1,\eta}\rangle_{X_{\eta}/\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}_{\eta}}

for each
P\in S^{(1)}
.

Let
\theta_{\eta}={\psi^{\prime}_{\eta}}^{-1}\circ\psi_{\eta}
and
\theta_{P}={\psi^{\prime}_{P}}^{-1}\circ\psi_{P}
for each
P\in S^{(1)}
. Then we have canonical isomorphisms

\theta_{\eta}\colon\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}}\times_{S}\eta\to\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}\times_{S}\eta

and

\theta_{P}\colon\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}}\times_{S}\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,P}\to\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}\times_{S}\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,P}

for each
P\in S^{(1)}
, and a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}}\times_{S}\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}\times_{S}\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}}\times_{S}\eta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}\times_{S}\eta.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{\eta}}

for each
P\in S^{(1)}
.

Since
S
is a noetherian normal integral scheme, and
\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}
are invertible
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules, we have

\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}}=\bigcap_{P\in S^{(1)}}\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}}\times_{S}\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,P}

and

\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}=\bigcap_{P\in S^{(1)}}\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}\times_{S}\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,P},

where the two intersections are taken in
\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}}\times_{S}\eta
and
\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}\times_{S}\eta
respectively.
Thus
\theta_{\eta}
restricts to a canonical isomorphism

\theta_{\mathbf{V},\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}\colon\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}.

It is easy to see that
\theta_{\mathbf{V},\mathbf{V}}=\id
and
\theta_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime},\mathbf{V}^{\prime\prime}}\circ\theta_{\mathbf{V},\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}=\theta_{\mathbf{V},\mathbf{V}^{\prime\prime}}
.
∎

If
S
is not irreducible, then by Lemma
2.1
we can apply the above construction over each irreducible component of
S
. Then we can also get a
\mathcal{J}
on
S
.

If
d=0
, we directly set
\langle\mathcal{L}\rangle_{X/S}\coloneq\textnormal{Nm}_{X/S}(\mathcal{L})
.

We summarize our results in this subsection as the following theorem.

Theorem 4.13
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a surjective projective morphism of noetherian schemes of pure relative dimension
d
. Suppose that
S
is normal. Then we can associate any
d+1

f
-sufficiently ample invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules
\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}
with the invertible
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{J}
, and denote it by
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
.

4.3.
Deligne pairing of general invertible sheaves

For simplicity, we introduce some notations.

Definition 4.14
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a surjective projective morphism. Suppose that
S
is integral and normal. Let
\eta
be the generic point of
S
, and
X_{\eta}=X\times_{S}\eta=f^{-1}(\eta)
. We use
P
to denote a point in
S^{(1)}
, and let
A=\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,P}
and
X_{A}=X\times_{S}\Spec A
. For an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{M}
, we define

\mathcal{M}_{\eta}=\mathcal{M}|_{\eta}=\mathcal{M}\times_{S}\eta

and

\mathcal{M}_{P}=\mathcal{M}|_{\Spec A}=\mathcal{M}\times_{S}\Spec A.

For an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{L}
, we define

\mathcal{L}_{\eta}=\mathcal{L}|_{X_{\eta}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{L}_{P}=\mathcal{L}|_{X_{A}}.

We present the above notations in the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}.}

From the proof of Proposition
4.12
, we can deduce the following propositions.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a surjective projective morphism of noetherian schemes of pure relative dimension
d
. Suppose that
S
is integral and normal, and that
\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}
are
d+1

f
-sufficiently ample invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules. Then we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n+1}\rangle_{X/S,\,P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n+1}\rangle_{X/S,\,\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,P},\mathcal{L}_{2,P},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n+1,P}\rangle_{X_{A}/\Spec A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,\eta},\mathcal{L}_{2,\eta},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n+1,\eta}\rangle_{X_{\eta}/\eta},}

where
P\in S^{(1)}
, two vertical arrows are canonical isomorphisms, and two bottom objects are Deligne pairings defined in
[
Gar00
]
.

The following proposition explains why we call our construction “Deligne pairing” and justifies our notation.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a surjective projective morphism of noetherian schemes of pure relative dimension
d
. Suppose that
S
is normal,
f
is of finite Tor-dimension, and that
\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}
are
d+1

f
-sufficiently ample invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules. Then our
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
is identical to the Deligne pairing defined in
[
Gar00
]
.

Proof.

We can assume that
S
is integral, and let
\eta
be the generic point of
S
. Let
\mathcal{M}
be the Deligne pairing of
\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}
defined by
[
Gar00
]
. Then we have

\mathcal{M}_{\eta}=\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,\eta},\mathcal{L}_{2,\eta},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1,\eta}\rangle_{X_{\eta}/\eta}

and

\mathcal{M}_{P}=\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,P},\mathcal{L}_{2,P},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1,P}\rangle_{X_{A}/\Spec A},

where both
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,\eta},\mathcal{L}_{2,\eta},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1,\eta}\rangle_{X_{\eta}/\eta}
and
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,P},\mathcal{L}_{2,P},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1,P}\rangle_{X_{A}/\Spec A}
are Deligne pairings defined by
[
Gar00
]
. Moreover, we have

\mathcal{M}=\bigcap_{P\in S^{(1)}}\mathcal{M}_{P},

where the intersection is taken in
\mathcal{M}_{\eta}
.

Let
\mathcal{N}=\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
be our Deligne pairing. Then by Proposition
4.26
,
\mathcal{N}_{\eta}=\mathcal{M}_{\eta}
,
\mathcal{N}_{P}=\mathcal{M}_{P}
for every
P\in S^{(1)}
, and

\mathcal{N}=\bigcap_{P\in S^{(1)}}\mathcal{N}_{P}=\bigcap_{P\in S^{(1)}}\mathcal{M}_{P}=\mathcal{M}.\qed

From the above reasoning, we can summarize the following very useful proposition.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
S
be a normal integral scheme, and let
\eta
be the generic point of
S
. Let
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
be two invertible
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules. Suppose that we have isomorphisms
\varphi_{\eta}\colon\mathcal{M}_{\eta}\to\mathcal{N}_{\eta}
and
\varphi_{P}\colon\mathcal{M}_{P}\to\mathcal{N}_{P}
for every
P\in S^{(1)}
. Moreover, for every
P\in S^{(1)}
, they satisfy the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}_{\eta}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\eta}}

Then
\varphi_{\eta}
restricts to an isomorphism
\varphi\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
.

Definition 4.18
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a projective morphism of noetherian schemes. Define
\Pic(X)_{f\text{-s.a}}
to be the subset of isomorphism classes of
f
-sufficiently ample invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules of
\Pic(X)
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a surjective projective morphism of noetherian schemes of pure relative dimension
d
. Suppose that
S
is normal. Then Theorem
4.13
defines a map

(4.8)

\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}\colon\Pic(X)^{d+1}_{f\text{-s.a}}\to\Pic(S).

Note that if
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\in\Pic(X)_{f\text{-s.a}}
, then
\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\in\Pic(X)_{f\text{-s.a}}
.

Proposition 4.19
.

The map (
4.8
) is multi-additive.

Proof.

We can assume that
S
is integral. Let
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
be an arbitrary integer, and let

\mathcal{M}=\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{i},\ldots\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}\otimes\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i},\ldots\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}

and

\mathcal{N}=\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i},\ldots\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S},

where all the terms are the same except the
i
-th term. Let
\eta
be the generic point of
S
.
Then we have a canonical isomorphism

\begin{split}\mathcal{N}_{\eta}&=\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,\eta},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{i,\eta}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i,\eta},\ldots\mathcal{L}_{d+1,\eta}\rangle_{X_{\eta}/\eta}\\
&=\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,\eta},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{i,\eta},\ldots\mathcal{L}_{d+1,\eta}\rangle_{X_{\eta}/\eta}\\
&\phantom{\mathrel{=}\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,\eta},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{i,\eta},}\otimes\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,\eta},\ldots,\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i,\eta},\ldots\mathcal{L}_{d+1,\eta}\rangle_{X_{\eta}/\eta}\\
&=\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{i},\ldots\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S,\eta}\otimes\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i},\ldots\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S,\eta}\\
&=\mathcal{M}_{\eta}.\end{split}

We denote this isomorphism by
\Sigma_{i,\eta}\colon\mathcal{M}_{\eta}\to\mathcal{N}_{\eta}
. For every
P\in S^{(1)}
, let
A=\mathcal{O}_{S,P}
, and then we have a canonical isomorphism

\begin{split}\mathcal{N}_{P}&=\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,P},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{i,P}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i,P},\ldots\mathcal{L}_{d+1,P}\rangle_{X_{A}/\Spec A}\\
&=\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,P},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{i,P},\ldots\mathcal{L}_{d+1,P}\rangle_{X_{A}/\Spec A}\\
&\phantom{\mathrel{=}\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,P},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{i,P},}\otimes\langle\mathcal{L}_{1,\eta},\ldots,\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i,\eta},\ldots\mathcal{L}_{d+1,\eta}\rangle_{X_{A}/\Spec A}\\
&=\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{i},\ldots\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S,P}\otimes\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i},\ldots\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S,P}\\
&=\mathcal{M}_{P}.\end{split}

We denote this isomorphism by
\Sigma_{i,P}\colon\mathcal{M}_{P}\to\mathcal{N}_{P}
. Moreover, for every
P\in S^{(1)}
, by
[
Gar00
]
Theorem 4.2.6, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}_{\eta}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{i,P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{i,\eta}}

By Proposition
4.17
,
\Sigma_{i,\eta}
restricts to a canonical isomorphism
