[s_{2}]_{X/S,\mathrm{i}}\colon\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}\to\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{Y_{2}/S},

[s_{2}]_{X/S,\mathrm{ii}}\colon\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}\to\langle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{Y_{2}/S},

and

[s_{2}]_{X/S,\mathrm{iii}}\colon\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}\to\langle\mathcal{L}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{Y_{2}/S}.

Note that
[s_{2}]_{X/S,\mathrm{i}}
,
[s_{2}]_{X/S,\mathrm{ii}}
,
[s_{2}]_{X/S,\mathrm{iii}}
,
\psi
, and
\varphi
are all isomorphisms. By inductive hypothesis,
\psi
is an isometry. So it suffices to prove that

\log\|[s_{2}]_{X/S,\mathrm{iii}}\|=[s_{2}]_{X/S,\mathrm{i}}+[s_{2}]_{X/S,\mathrm{ii}},

which follows from Lemma
8.6
and the fact that

c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\|\cdot\|_{1}\otimes\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{1})=c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{1},\|\cdot\|_{1})+c_{1}(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{1}).

Since locally every invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module is the difference of two
f
-ample invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module, and by Lemma
7.3
some positive power of an
f
-ample invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module admits an
f
-regular global section. Thus the above claim allows us to endow the Deligne pairing
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
of general hermitian line bundles
\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}
with a smooth metric, regardless of whether
\mathcal{L}_{1}
admits regular sections. And we see that
\Sigma_{i}\,(1\leqslant i\leqslant d+1)
,
\gamma_{\phi}
(
\phi
is a permutation of the set {2,3,…,d+1}), and (
8.3
) are all isometries. Moreover, since both sides of (
8.4
) are linear, we can strengthen Lemma
8.6
as follows.

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
s_{2}
be an
f
-regular global sections of
\mathcal{L}_{2}
and
Y_{2}=Z(s_{2})

If we endow
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
with the metric we constructed above, then

\log\|[s_{2}]_{X/S}\|=-\int_{X/S}\log\|s_{2}\|_{2}\prod_{i\neq 2}c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{i},\|\cdot\|_{i}),

where
[s_{2}]_{X/S}\colon\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}\to\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{3},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{Y_{2}/S}
is the isomorphism in Theorem
5.7
for
D=Z(s_{2})
.

It remains to show that
\gamma_{(12)}
is an isometry, where
(12)
denotes the transposition. Since the problem is local, and
\Sigma_{1}
is an isometry, we can assume that
\mathcal{L}_{1}
admits an
f
-regular sequence
s_{1}
, and let
Y_{1}=Z(s_{1})
. By Theorem
6.4
, we have the following commutative diagram

(8.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\dots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{L}_{2},\dots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{Y_{1}/S}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{L}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{3},\dots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{(12)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}s_{1}{]}_{X/S,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}s_{1}{]}_{X/S,2}}

By Lemma
8.8
,
\log\|[s_{1}]_{X/S,1}\|=\log\|[s_{1}]_{X/S,2}\|
. Therefore,
\gamma_{(12)}
is an isometry.
∎

Let
U\subseteq S
be the maximal open subset over which
f
is flat. By
[
YZ24
]
Theorem 4.2.3, wan can construct a canonical metric
\|\cdot\|_{X_{U}/U}
on
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n+1}\rangle|_{X_{U}/U}
. By comparing the constructions in
[
YZ24
]
§4.2, §4.3, and our paper, we can see that the restriction of our metric
\|\cdot\|
on
U
is exactly
\|\cdot\|_{X_{U}/U}
. In addition, if the metrics on
\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}
are only continuous integrable,
[
YZ24
]
Theorem 4.2.3 shows that we can still construct a metric on
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{n+1}\rangle_{X_{U}/U}
, which is continuous integrable. Since a continuous integrable metric is a uniform limit of smooth metrics, it is not hard to extend our results to continuous integrable metrics, and we omit the details.

The following theorems show that the canonical metric is compatible with functorial properties.

Theorem 8.9
.

Let
X
and
S
quasi-projective schemes over
\mathbb{C}
, and let
f\colon X\to S
be a surjective projective morphism over
\mathbb{C}
of pure relative dimension
d
. Suppose that
S
is normal. Let
(\mathcal{L}_{1},\|\cdot\|_{1}),(\mathcal{L}_{2},\|\cdot\|_{2}),\ldots,(\mathcal{L}_{d+1},\|\cdot\|_{d+1}),(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{1}),(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{2}),\ldots,(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{d+1},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{d+1})
be invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules endowed with smooth metrics. For each
1\leqslant i\leqslant d+1
, let
u_{i}\colon\mathcal{L}_{i}\to\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i}
be an isometry. If we endow
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
and
\langle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
with the metrics we constructed in Theorem
8.5
. Then the following isomorphism of invertible
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules

\langle u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}\colon\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}\to\langle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}

is also an isometry.

Proof.

We prove the result by induction on
d
. Let
\|\cdot\|
and
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
denote the metrics on
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
and
\langle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}
respectively. If
d=0
, then for each nonzero rational section
s
of
\mathcal{L}
,

\log\|\textnormal{Nm}_{X/S}(u_{1}(s))\|^{\prime}=\int_{X/S}\log\|u_{1}(s)\|^{\prime}_{1}=\int_{X/S}\log\|s\|_{1}=\log\|\textnormal{Nm}_{X/S}(s)\|.

Thus
\|\cdot\|=\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
.

Suppose that
d>0
. We can assume that
\mathcal{L}_{1}
admits an
f
-regular section
s_{1}
. Let
Y=Z(s_{1})=Z(u_{1}(s_{1}))
. Then we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{L}_{2}|_{Y},\ldots,\ldots\mathcal{L}_{d+1}|_{Y}\rangle_{Y/S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2}|_{Y},\ldots\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{d+1}|_{{Y}}\rangle_{{Y}/S}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}s_{1}{]}_{X/S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle u_{1}{,}u_{2}{,}\ldots{,}u_{d+1}\rangle_{X/S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle u_{2}|_{Y}{,}\ldots{,}u_{d+1}|_{Y}\rangle_{Y/S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}u_{1}(s_{1}){]}_{X/S}}

By inductive hypothesis, the right vertical arrow is an isometry. Moreover,

\log\|[u_{1}(s_{1})]_{X/S}\|=-\int_{X/S}\log\|u_{1}(s_{1})\|^{\prime}_{1}\prod_{i=2}^{d+1}c_{1}(\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i},\|\cdot\|^{\prime}_{i})\\
=-\int_{X/S}\log\|s_{1}\|_{1}\prod_{i=2}^{d+1}c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{i},\|\cdot\|_{i})=\log\|[s_{1}]_{X/S}\|.

Thus the left vertical arrow is also an isometry.
∎

Theorem 8.10
.

Let
X
and
S
quasi-projective schemes over
\mathbb{C}
, and let
f\colon X\to S
be a surjective projective morphism over
\mathbb{C}
of pure relative dimension
d
. Suppose that
S
is integral and normal. Let
(\mathcal{L}_{1},\|\cdot\|_{1}),(\mathcal{L}_{2},\|\cdot\|_{2}),\ldots,(\mathcal{L}_{d},\|\cdot\|_{d})
be invertible
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules endowed with smooth metrics, and let
(\mathcal{M},\|\cdot\|)
be an invertible
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules endowed with smooth metrics. We endow
f^{*}\mathcal{M}
with the smooth metric
f^{*}\|\cdot\|
. Let
\delta
be the intersection number of
\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d}
on the generic fiber of
f
(if
d=0
, then let
\delta
be the degree of the finite morphism
f
). If we endow
\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,f^{*}\mathcal{M},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d}\rangle_{X/S}
with the canonical metric, then the natural isomorphism

\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,f^{*}\mathcal{M},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d}\rangle_{X/S}\to\mathcal{M}^{\otimes\delta}

is an isometry.

Proof.

We prove the result by induction on
d
. If
d=0
, then for any nonzero rational section
s
of
\mathcal{M}
,

\log\|f^{*}s\|=\int_{X/S}\log f^{*}\|s\|=\delta\log\|s\|.

Suppose that
d>0
. Then we can assume that
\mathcal{L}_{1}
admits an
f
-regular section
s_{1}
. Let
Y=Z(s_{1})
. By properties of intersection numbers,
\delta
equals the intersection number of
\mathcal{L}_{2}|_{Y},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d}|_{Y}
on the generic fiber of
Y\to S
. Then we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{L}_{1},\ldots,f^{*}\mathcal{M},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d}\rangle_{X/S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{\otimes\delta}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{L}_{2}|_{Y},\ldots,f^{*}\mathcal{M}|_{Y},\ldots,\mathcal{L}_{d}|_{Y}\rangle_{Y/S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[}s_{1}{]}_{X/S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

By inductive hypothesis,
\psi
is an isometry. Moreover,

\log\|{[s_{1}]_{X/S}}\|=-\int_{X/S}\log|s_{1}\|_{1}f^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{M},\|\cdot\|)\prod_{i=2}^{d}c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{i},\|\cdot\|_{i})=0,

since
c_{1}(\mathcal{M},\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{m}})
is a smooth form on
S
. Therefore,
\varphi
is also an isometry.
∎
